In April 2018, Johnson travelled to Italy to attend a party at the [Palazzo Terranova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palazzo_Terranova), owned by former [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) agent [Alexander Lebedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Lebedev) and hosted by his son [Evgeny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evgeny_Lebedev). He travelled without security protection or other officials,[^cite_note-Culbertson_2022_a393-348] and did not document the trip, which led to accusations of Johnson having misled parliament.[^cite_note-Maidment_2022_r017-349] In 2023, it was revealed that Lord Simon McDonald, the most senior civil servant of his department, was unaware of the trip. Johnson stated that "no government business was discussed" at the event as far as he was aware.[^cite_note-The_Independent_2022_e651-350][^cite_note-Financial_Times_2023_b191-351] Lebedev's villa was monitored by the Italian secret service, who suspected it to be used for espionage activities.[^cite_note-Crerar_2023_n661-352] Johnson granted a peerage to [Evgeny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evgeny_Lebedev) in 2020, against the advice of the [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6), and met with criticism over security concerns.[^cite_note-Freeman_2022_b237-353][^cite_note-BBC_News_2022_t584-354][^cite_note-Reporters_2022_o388-355]

In July 2018, three days after the cabinet had its meeting to agree on a [Brexit strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_future_relationship_between_the_United_Kingdom_and_the_European_Union),[^cite_note-bbc_44749993-356] Johnson, along with Brexit secretary [David Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Davis_\(British_politician\)),[^cite_note-theguardian_2018jul08_david-357] resigned his post.[^cite_note-theguardian_2018jul09_boris-358]

### Return to the backbenches: 2018–2019

Johnson returned to the role of a [backbench MP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backbench_MP). In July, he delivered a resignation speech, stating "it is not too late to save Brexit".[^cite_note-bbc_44871629-359] In January 2019, Johnson came under criticism for remarks he had made during the 2016 Leave campaign regarding the prospect of Turkish accession to the European Union; he denied making such remarks.[^cite_note-itv_20190118_boris-360] In March 2019, he was criticised for saying that expenditure on investigating historic allegations of child abuse was money "spaffed up the wall".[^cite_note-belfasttelegraph_37909038-361][^cite_note-bbc_47560192-362]

#### Journalism

In July 2018, Johnson signed a 12‑month contract to write articles for the [Telegraph Media Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telegraph_Media_Group).[^cite_note-gov-363] The [Advisory Committee on Business Appointments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advisory_Committee_on_Business_Appointments) (ACOBA) reported that this was a breach of the [Ministerial Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministerial_Code).[^cite_note-gov-363][^cite_note-bbc_45121976-364] Johnson was ordered to apologise for failing to declare £50,000 of earnings. The [Parliamentary Commissioner for Standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_Commissioner_for_Standards) found the errors were not inadvertent, and that Johnson had failed on nine occasions to make declarations within the rules.[^cite_note-businessinsider_20181206-365]

In September 2018, Johnson wrote: "We have opened ourselves to perpetual political blackmail. We have wrapped a suicide vest around the British constitution – and handed the detonator to [Michel Barnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Barnier)." Senior Tories heavily criticised him, with [Alan Duncan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Duncan) of the Foreign Office vowing to ensure the comments marked "the political end of Boris Johnson".[^cite_note-theguardian_2018sep09_boris-366][^cite_note-independent_8529621-367] In April 2019, the [Independent Press Standards Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Press_Standards_Organisation) ruled that a claim in a 6 January 2019 article in *The Daily Telegraph*, "The British people won't be scared into backing a woeful Brexit deal nobody voted for", authored by Johnson,[^cite_note-Telegraph-2019-01-368] that a no-deal [Brexit](41688778.md) was "by some margin preferred by the British public" was false, and "represented a failure to take care over the accuracy of the article in breach of Clause 1 (i)" of its guidelines, and required that a correction be published.[^cite_note-IPSO-2019-04-369]

#### 2019 Conservative Party leadership election

Main article: [2019 Conservative Party leadership election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Conservative_Party_leadership_election)



The logo used by Johnson's leadership campaign in 2019

On 12 June 2019, Johnson launched his campaign for the [Conservative Party leadership election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Conservative_Party_leadership_election), saying, "we must leave the EU on 31 October."[^cite_note-spectator_20190612-370] On the campaign trail, Johnson warned of "catastrophic consequences for voter trust in politics" if the government pushed the EU for further delays. He advocated removing the [backstop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backstop_agreement) from any Brexit deal. On 25 and 26 August, he announced plans to retain £7 or £9 billion of the £39 billion divorce payment the UK was due to transfer to the EU upon withdrawal.[^cite_note-thetimes_20190825-371][^cite_note-sky_11793797-372] Johnson initially pledged to cut income tax for earners of more than £50,000 but backed away from this plan in June 2019 after coming under criticism in a televised [BBC](19344654.md) debate.[^cite_note-independent_8964566-373]

Johnson was elected leader with 92,153 votes (66%) to Hunt's 46,656 (34%).[^cite_note-theguardian_2019jul23_boris-374]

## Premiership (2019–2022)

Main article: [Premiership of Boris Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiership_of_Boris_Johnson)



Johnson giving his first speech as Prime Minister on 24 July 2019

### First term (July–December 2019)

Wikisource logo

English [Wikisource](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikisource) has original text related to this article:&#x20;

**[Boris Johnson: First Speech as Prime Minister](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/en\:Boris%20Johnson:%20First%20Speech%20as%20Prime%20Minister)**

On 24 July 2019, the day following Johnson's election as [Conservative Party leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Conservative_Party_\(UK\)), Queen [Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_II) accepted [Theresa May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theresa_May)'s resignation and appointed Johnson as prime minister. This made Johnson the first prime minister to be born outside [British territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_evolution_of_the_British_Empire).[^cite_note-Auto4S-21-375] Johnson appointed [Dominic Cummings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominic_Cummings) as his senior advisor.[^cite_note-Auto4S-22-376]

#### Brexit policy



Johnson discussing Brexit with French president [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) in Paris



Johnson signing the Brexit Withdrawal Agreement

In his [first speech as PM](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Boris%20Johnson:%20First%20Speech%20as%20Prime%20Minister), Johnson said that the United Kingdom would leave the European Union on 31 October 2019 with or [without a deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_deal_Brexit), and promised to remove the [Irish backstop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_backstop) from the withdrawal agreement.[^cite_note-theguardian_2019jul24_boris-377][^cite_note-Auto4S-23-378] Johnson declared his intention to [re-open negotiations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_negotiations_in_2019), but talks did not immediately resume as the EU refused to accept Johnson's condition that the backstop be removed.[^cite_note-independent_9026001-379] On 28 August 2019, UK and EU negotiators agreed to resume regular meetings.[^cite_note-Auto4S-24-380]

Also on 28 August 2019, Johnson declared he had asked the Queen to [prorogue Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prorogation_in_the_United_Kingdom) from 10 September, reducing the time in which Parliament could block a no-deal Brexit and causing a [political controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_British_prorogation_controversy).[^cite_note-theguardian_2019aug28_chancellor-381] The Queen at [Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom) approved prorogation later the same day, and it began on 10 September, scheduled to last until 14 October.[^cite_note-theguardian_2019aug28_spending-382] Some suggested[^cite_note-co_20190828_backstop-383] that this prorogation amounted to a [self-coup](1221872.md), and on 31 August 2019, protests occurred throughout the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-bbc_49534940-384][^cite_note-theguardian_2019aug31_bodmin-385] By 2 September 2019, three separate court cases challenging Johnson's action were in progress or scheduled to take place,[^cite_note-reuters_idUSKCN1VK1LK-386] and on 11 September, three Scottish judges ruled the prorogation of the UK Parliament to be unlawful.[^cite_note-theguardian_2019sep11_scottish-387][^cite_note-bbc_49661855-388] On 12 September, Johnson denied lying to the Queen over suspension of the Parliament, while a Belfast Court rejected claims that his Brexit plans would have a negative impact on Northern Ireland's peace policy.[^cite_note-cbs6albany_20190912-389] On 24 September, the Supreme Court ruled unanimously that Johnson's advice to prorogue Parliament was unlawful, and therefore the prorogation was rendered null.[^cite_note-theguardian_2019sep24_boris-390][^cite_note-bbc_49810261-391]

When Parliament resumed on 3 September 2019, Johnson indicated he would call a general election under the [Fixed-term Parliaments Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed-term_Parliaments_Act) after opposition and rebel Conservative MPs successfully voted against the government to take control of the order of business to prevent a no-deal exit.[^cite_note-co_20190903_brexit-392] Despite government opposition, the [Benn Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benn_Act), a bill to block a no-deal exit, passed the Commons on 4 September 2019, causing Johnson to propose a general election on 15 October.[^cite_note-bbc_49580185-393] His motion was unsuccessful as it failed to command the support of two-thirds of the House.[^cite_note-bbc_49584907-394]

In October 2019, following bilateral talks between Johnson and [Taoiseach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoiseach) [Leo Varadkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Varadkar),[^cite_note-bbc_49995133-395] the UK and EU agreed to a [revised deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_withdrawal_agreement#Revisions_in_2019), which [replaced the backstop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_and_the_Irish_border#2019_renegotiation:_New_protocol) with a new [Northern Ireland Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Protocol).[^cite_note-bbcNewDeal-396]

#### First Cabinet

Main article: [First Johnson ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Johnson_ministry)



Johnson holding his first cabinet meeting at [10 Downing Street](226294.md), 25 July 2019

Johnson [appointed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_British_cabinet_formation) his [Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Johnson_ministry) on 24 July 2019,[^cite_note-BBC25-397] dismissing 11 senior ministers and accepting the resignation of six others.[^cite_note-thetimes_20190725-398][^cite_note-theguardian_2019jul24_philip-399] The mass dismissal was the most extensive postwar Cabinet reorganisation without a change in the ruling party.[^cite_note-independent_9019726-400][^cite_note-theguardian_2019jul25_cabinet-401]

Johnson made [Dominic Raab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominic_Raab) the [First Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Secretary_of_State) and foreign secretary.

[Sajid Javid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sajid_Javid) became [Chancellor of the Exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer)

[Priti Patel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priti_Patel) became [Home Secretary](149104.md).

#### Loss of working majority

On 3 September 2019, [Phillip Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phillip_Lee_\(politician\)) [crossed the floor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crossed_the_floor) to the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\)) following a disagreement with Johnson's [Brexit](41688778.md) policy. This left the government without a [working majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_majority) in the House of Commons.[^cite_note-independent_9090356-402] Later that day, [21 Conservative MPs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_2019_suspension_of_rebel_Conservative_MPs) had the party [whip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whip_\(politics\)) withdrawn for defying party orders and supporting an opposition motion.[^cite_note-politicshome_106279-403] (The whip was restored to 10 former Conservative ministers on 29 October.[^cite_note-bbc_50221856-404])



Johnson giving a speech in December 2019 after the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election)

On 5 September 2019, Johnson's brother [Jo Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo_Johnson) resigned from the government and announced that he would step down as MP, describing his position as "torn between family and national interest".[^cite_note-bbc2-405] Two days later, [Amber Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amber_Rudd) resigned as [Secretary of State for Work and Pensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Work_and_Pensions) and from the Conservative Party, describing the withdrawal of the party whip as an "assault on decency and democracy".[^cite_note-Auto4S-25-406]

#### 2019 general election

Main article: [2019 United Kingdom general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election)

In October 2019, Parliament was dissolved, and an election called for 12 December. The election resulted in the Conservative Party winning 43.6% of the vote and a parliamentary landslide majority of 80 seats.[^cite_note-bbc_50774061-407] A key slogan used in the Conservative campaign was "[Get Brexit Done](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Get_Brexit_Done)".[^cite_note-time_5749478-408]

### Second term (December 2019 – September 2022)

#### Second Cabinet

Main articles: [Second Johnson ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Johnson_ministry), [2020 British cabinet reshuffle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_British_cabinet_reshuffle), and [2021 British cabinet reshuffle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_British_cabinet_reshuffle)



Johnson with his cabinet in 2022



Johnson appointed [Rishi Sunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishi_Sunak) (right) as [Chancellor of the Exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer) in the [February 2020 cabinet reshuffle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_British_cabinet_reshuffle).

Johnson [reshuffled his cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_British_cabinet_reshuffle) in February 2020.[^cite_note-bbc_51491662-409] Five Cabinet ministers were sacked, including the [Northern Ireland secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_secretary) [Julian Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_Smith_\(politician\)), a decision that was criticised by several politicians and commentators following his success in restoring the [Northern Ireland Executive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Executive) devolved government.[^cite_note-Walker-410] [Chancellor of the Exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer) [Sajid Javid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sajid_Javid) resigned from the Cabinet and was replaced by [Rishi Sunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishi_Sunak); Javid later returned to Johnson's Cabinet as [Secretary of State for Health and Social Care](356531.md) in June 2021 following the resignation of [Matt Hancock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Hancock).[^cite_note-JavResign-411]

Johnson reshuffled his cabinet again in September 2021. Changes included the dismissal of [Education Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Education) [Gavin Williamson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gavin_Williamson) who had received significant criticism for his handling of disruption to education during the COVID-19 pandemic. [Dominic Raab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominic_Raab) was moved from foreign secretary to [deputy prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom) and [justice secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Justice). Raab was replaced as foreign secretary by [Liz Truss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liz_Truss).[^cite_note-bbc_58571935-412][^cite_note-sky_12408644-413]

#### COVID-19 pandemic

Main articles: [British government response to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_government_response_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic) and [COVID-19 vaccination programme in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_programme_in_the_United_Kingdom)

See also: [COVID-19 contracts in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_contracts_in_the_United_Kingdom) and [COVID-19 pandemic in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_Kingdom)



Johnson delivering a press conference on [COVID-19](63030231.md), 31 July 2020

The [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_Kingdom) emerged as a serious crisis within the first few months of Johnson's second term.[^cite_note-theguardian_2020mar20_london-414] Johnson's non-attendance of five [COBR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COBR) briefings during the early months and the failure of the UK government to prepare for and control the outbreak has been criticised.[^cite_note-Auto4S-26-415][^cite_note-theguardian_2020apr19_michael-416][^cite_note-theguardian_2020apr18_how-417] The UK was among the last major European states to close schools, ban public events and order a [lockdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns).[^cite_note-dw-20200401-418][^cite_note-imperial-20200330-419] This response is thought by some scientists to have contributed to the UK's high death toll from COVID-19, which was the highest in Europe and among the highest in the world.[^cite_note-reuters_2-420][^cite_note-421]

*Reuters* reported that scientists were critical of Johnson both for acting too slowly to stop the spread of COVID-19 and for mishandling the government's response;[^cite_note-macaskill-422] *Politico* quoted [Chief Medical Officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Medical_Officer_for_England) [Chris Whitty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Whitty) as saying that an earlier initial lockdown would have significantly lowered the death toll.[^cite_note-Auto4S-27-423] The *[BMJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMJ)* published several editorials critical of the policies adopted during the country's public health response.[^cite_note-bmj_20200515-424][^cite_note-bmj_20210201-425] Johnson's public communication over the virus and the UK's test and trace system were also criticised.[^cite_note-theatlantic_608065-426][^cite_note-macaskill-422]

On 3 March 2020 Johnson claimed to have shaken hands with COVID-19 patients in hospital on the same day that [SAGE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Advisory_Group_for_Emergencies) had advised the government to warn the public not to shake hands and minimise physical contact,[^cite_note-theguardian_2020may05_boris-427][^cite_note-sky_11983606-428] though it was unclear whether the hospital he visited actually contained any coronavirus patients.[^cite_note-NORTH-429] He continued to shake hands publicly in the following days.[^cite_note-ft_20200505-430] On 23 March, a [COVID-19 lockdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdown_in_the_United_Kingdom) was imposed throughout the UK, except for a few limited purposes, backed up by [new legal powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Health_Protection_\(Coronavirus,_Restrictions\)_\(England\)_Regulations_2020).[^cite_note-cnn_20200323_uk-431]



Johnson giving a speech in April 2020 after recovering from [COVID-19](63030231.md)

On 27 March, it was announced that Johnson had tested positive for [COVID-19](63030231.md).[^cite_note-CoronaBJ-432] On 5 April, he was admitted to a hospital.[^cite_note-bbc_52177125-433] The next day, he was moved to the hospital's intensive care unit.[^cite_note-bbc_52192604-434][^cite_note-IT_Reactions_Guardian-435] Johnson left intensive care on 9 April,[^cite_note-reuters_idUSKCN21R34I-436] and left hospital three days later to recuperate at [Chequers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chequers).[^cite_note-bbc_52262012-437] After a fortnight at Chequers, he returned to Downing Street on 26 April.[^cite_note-bbc_52431913-438] Johnson later said that he had been given [emergency oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen_therapy) while in intensive care, and that doctors had made preparations in case he died.[^cite_note-nbcnews_1198871-439]

A [scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominic_Cummings_scandal) in May 2020 involved Johnson's chief political advisor [Dominic Cummings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominic_Cummings), who made a trip with his family to [Durham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durham,_England) during the March 2020 lockdown while experiencing COVID-19 symptoms. Both Cummings and Johnson rejected widespread calls that Cummings resign.[^cite_note-theguardian_2020may26_a-440][^cite_note-bbc_52793529-441][^cite_note-slate_20200526-442] Johnson's defence of Cummings and his refusal to sack him caused a widespread backlash.[^cite_note-Auto4S-28-443] This resulted in a loss of confidence in the government and specifically its response to the pandemic, referred to as 'the Cummings effect' in *[The Lancet](463899.md)*. Concerns were raised in the study that this could affect the public's compliance with pandemic restrictions.[^cite_note-Auto4S-29-444][^cite_note-co_20200806_dominic-445]

The Johnson ministry was [accused of cronyism in their assignment of contracts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controversies_regarding_COVID-19_contracts_in_the_United_Kingdom) related to the pandemic response. Procurement of government contracts for key COVID-19 supplies became less transparent as a result of emergency measures bypassing the usual competitive tendering process.[^cite_note-PogCal-446][^cite_note-GuardChums-447] In October 2020 Johnson conceded that the UK's [test and trace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_Test_and_Trace) system and its specially developed [contact tracing app](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_COVID-19), which had been criticised for their cost and operational issues, had caused "frustrations".[^cite_note-macaskill-422][^cite_note-theguardian_2020sep14_utter-448][^cite_note-bbc_54650029-449]



Johnson hosts virtual G7 meeting in February 2021.

Johnson reportedly resisted calls from SAGE and within the government to enact a second lockdown throughout September as COVID-19 infections rose.[^cite_note-\:0a-450][^cite_note-forbes_20210316_report-451] In April 2021 Johnson denied allegations he had said he would rather "[let the bodies pile high in their thousands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Let_the_bodies_pile_high_in_their_thousands)" on 30 October 2020.[^cite_note-co_20210426_let-452][^cite_note-bbc_56890714-453][^cite_note-itv_20210426_robert-454] The government enacted a second national lockdown on 31 October.[^cite_note-theguardian4-455] Throughout December 2020 COVID-19 cases across the UK rose significantly, straining emergency services and hospitals.[^cite_note-bloomberg-456][^cite_note-bbc_55362681-457] In response, the government enacted further restrictions to large parts of southern and eastern England and on 21 December shortened a planned household mixing period over Christmas.[^cite_note-cnn2-458]

Britain began [its COVID-19 vaccination programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_the_United_Kingdom) in December 2020.[^cite_note-bmj_20210218-459] Half of UK adults had received at least their first vaccine dose by 20 March 2021.[^cite_note-bbc_56469092-460] A third lockdown for the whole of England was introduced on 6 January 2021.[^cite_note-bbc_55554550-461] Record numbers of infections and daily deaths were recorded in the UK throughout January, and the government began exploring quarantine procedures on arrival.[^cite_note-bbc_55757790-462] Johnson said he was "deeply sorry" and "take\[s] full responsibility" as the UK passed 100,000 deaths from COVID-19, the first European country to do so, on 26 January.[^cite_note-bbc_55814751-463]



Johnson with US president [Joe Biden](145422.md) at the [G7 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/47th_G7_summit) in Cornwall, 10 June 2021

In July 2021 Johnson announced that most generalised public health restrictions in England would be lifted and replaced by recommendations. This took place despite an increase in cases driven by the [Delta variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_variant).[^cite_note-apnews_180720-464][^cite_note-cnbc_20210719_uk-465] In September 2021 Johnson was pictured in a cabinet meeting, with "at least 30 people crammed shoulder-to-shoulder", without anyone wearing masks and with all windows apparently closed, contradicting government advice.[^cite_note-theguardian_2021sep17_boris-466] Johnson was also photographed without a face mask during a visit to a hospital in November.[^cite_note-sky_12464422-467]

In December 2021 more stringent "Plan B" restrictions for England were put forward, a partial renewal of previous measures due to the increased incidence of the [SARS-CoV-2 Omicron variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant). These proposals included face coverings to be required in more public settings, guidance to [work from home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Work_from_home) wherever possible, and requirements of [COVID-19 passports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID_passports_in_the_United_Kingdom) to enter certain venues.[^cite_note-bbc_59585307-468] The government experienced the largest rebellion of Conservative MPs during Johnson's premiership, in opposition to these measures.[^cite_note-thetimes_20211214-469][^cite_note-co_20211214_boris-470]

#### Immigration

Further information: [Modern immigration to the United Kingdom](1247786.md)

In 2019, Johnson promised to reduce net migration to the United Kingdom (the number of people immigrating minus the number emigrating) below 250,000 per year by the next election.[^cite_note-sky_12883019-471] In 2021 net migration to the UK was 488,000,[^cite_note-sky_12888478-472][^cite_note-ons.gov.uk-25-05-2023-473] up from 184,000 in 2019 before the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-independent_2452365-474] Most of the migrants came from non-EU countries. As a result of Brexit, more EU nationals left the UK than arrived.[^cite_note-ons.gov.uk-25-05-2023-473]

In 2021, Johnson's government launched a [scheme for Hongkongers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_National_\(Overseas\)#Expanded_access_to_UK_residence) following introduction of the [2020 Hong Kong national security law](64033197.md), with more than 180,000 Hong Kong British National (Overseas) (BN(O)) status holders arriving in the UK or being granted in-country extension by March 2024.[^cite_note-www_20240613-475]

Long-term net migration to the UK reached a record high of 764,000 in 2022,[^cite_note-bbc_48785695-476] with immigration at 1.26 million and emigration at 493,000.[^cite_note-lbc_20240523-477]

#### Legislative agenda

At the [State Opening of Parliament on 11 May 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_State_Opening_of_Parliament), a range of proposed laws were announced, including the [Dissolution and Calling of Parliament Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_and_Calling_of_Parliament_Bill), which would restore the [royal prerogative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_prerogative) to dissolve Parliament; a [Higher Education (Freedom of Speech) Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_Education_\(Freedom_of_Speech\)_Bill) to combat [deplatforming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deplatforming) at universities;[^cite_note-Metro_11-05-2021-478] an [Online Safety Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_Safety_Act_2023) that would impose a statutory [duty of care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duty_of_care) on online companies and empower [Ofcom](411296.md) to block particular websites;[^cite_note-bbc_57071977-479] and an [Animal Welfare (Sentience) Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_Welfare_\(Sentience\)_Bill) that would legally recognise animal sentience. Further laws would introduce [mandatory voter identification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_identification_laws) at general elections, reform the national immigration system, and implement a [levelling up policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levelling_up_policy_of_the_Boris_Johnson_government) to reduce imbalances between areas.[^cite_note-Metro_11-05-2021-478]

#### 2021 Downing Street refurbishment controversy

Further information: [2021 Downing Street refurbishment controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Downing_Street_refurbishment_controversy)

In April 2021 Cummings alleged that Johnson had arranged for donors to "secretly pay" for renovations on the private residence at 11 Downing Street.[^cite_note-\:0b-480] On 27 April Johnson asked the Cabinet Secretary, [Simon Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Case), to hold a review about the refurbishment.[^cite_note-bbc_56883078-481] On 28 April, the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_\(United_Kingdom\)) announced it had opened a formal investigation.[^cite_note-bbc_56915307-482][^cite_note-theguardian_2021apr28_electoral-483] On the same day Johnson said that he had not broken any laws over the refurbishment and had met the requirements he was obliged to meet.[^cite_note-Auto4S-30-484] During [Prime Minister's Questions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister's_Questions), the leader of the opposition, [Keir Starmer](18681491.md), asked: "Who *initially* paid for the redecoration of his Downing Street flat?"; Johnson responded: "I paid for Downing Street's refurbishment personally."[^cite_note-parliament_20210428_debates-485]

On 28 May [Lord Geidt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Geidt) published a report on the allegations which concluded that Johnson did not breach the Ministerial Code and that no conflict, or reasonably perceived conflict, of interest arose. However, Lord Geidt expressed that it was "unwise" for Johnson to have proceeded without "more rigorous regard for how this would be funded".[^cite_note-theguardian_2021may28_boris-486][^cite_note-bbc_57280418-487] The Electoral Commission reported on 9 December that it found that the Conservative Party had failed to follow the law in not accurately reporting donations to the party from Lord Brownlow and imposed a £17,800 fine on the party.[^cite_note-theguardian_2021dec09_downing-488][^cite_note-rod21-489] *[The Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Herald_\(Glasgow\))* said the commission's report outlined how, in March, all the money paid by Brownlow and his company had been reimbursed, as had the payments made by the Conservative Party and Cabinet Office.[^cite_note-rod21-489] Downing Street had said at the time that the full cost of the works had been met personally by the prime minister.[^cite_note-rod21-489]

#### Owen Paterson controversy

See also: [Owen Paterson § Lobbying and breach of Commons advocacy rules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owen_Paterson#Lobbying_and_breach_of_Commons_advocacy_rules), and [United Kingdom parliamentary second jobs controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_parliamentary_second_jobs_controversy)

In November 2021 Johnson backed a motion to block the suspension of [Owen Paterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owen_Paterson), a Conservative MP found to have abused his position by the [independent standards commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_Commissioner_for_Standards) after undertaking paid lobbying.[^cite_note-reuters_20211103-490] The motion called for the creation of a new Conservative-majority committee to examine reforms of the standards investigation process.[^cite_note-bbc_59154221-491] Many Conservative MPs refused to support the motion, and 13 defied a [three-line whip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-line_whip) to vote against it.[^cite_note-independent_1950763-492] Following the announcement by opposition parties that they would boycott the new committee, and faced with a backlash in the media and from MPs of all parties, the government announced that a new vote would take place on whether Paterson should be suspended.[^cite_note-bbc_59163961-493] Paterson announced his resignation as an MP the same day.[^cite_note-bbc_59167783-494] A [by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_North_Shropshire_by-election) in Paterson's former constituency of [North Shropshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Shropshire_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\)) saw the [Liberal Democrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\)) candidate, [Helen Morgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Morgan_\(politician\)), overturn a Conservative majority of nearly 23,000,[^cite_note-bbc_59693102-495] the [seventh largest swing in United Kingdom by-election history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_by-election_records#Largest_swings).[^cite_note-sky_12497814-496]

#### Partygate scandal

Main article: [Partygate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partygate)



Downing Street, where some of the gatherings took place

[^cite_note-bbc12/4/2022-497]

In December 2021 reports emerged that [social gatherings of government and Conservative Party staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partygate) in Downing Street had taken place ahead of Christmas 2020 against COVID-19 regulations. Johnson denied these allegations.[^cite_note-independent_1971373-498][^cite_note-reuters_20211207-499] Following a leaked video showing Downing Street staff joking about a "fictional party", at a press conference rehearsal recorded days after one alleged party took place, Johnson apologised for the contents of the video and suggested he had been misled but had now ordered an inquiry.[^cite_note-theguardian_2021dec07_leaked-500][^cite_note-bbc_59580640-501]

On 10 January 2022, [ITV News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITV_News) reported that a planned party had taken place on 20 May 2020, during the first lockdown. ITV had obtained an email sent by principal private secretary [Martin Reynolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Reynolds_\(civil_servant\)) to staff inviting them to "socially distanced drinks" in the garden of No. 10.[^cite_note-BYOB-502] At the time, people outdoors were not allowed to meet more than one person from outside their household.[^cite_note-sky12/1/2022-503] Two eyewitnesses later alleged that Johnson and Symonds attended, contradicting Johnson's insistence in December 2021 that there were "no parties".[^cite_note-bbc_59946784-504][^cite_note-BYOB-502]

On 12 January, Johnson apologised to MPs in the Commons for "attending an event in the Downing Street garden during the first lockdown",[^cite_note-bbc_59961535-505][^cite_note-theguardian_2022jan12_boris-506] stating he believed it was "a work event".[^cite_note-itv_20220112_johnson-507] He said that MPs should await the outcome of the independent inquiry, led by senior civil servant [Sue Gray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Gray_\(civil_servant\)), which he said "will report as soon as possible".[^cite_note-CTQ-508] There were calls across the House for Johnson to resign.[^cite_note-CTQ-508][^cite_note-bbc12/1/2022-509] One week later, Conservative former minister [David Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Davis_\(British_politician\)) called for Johnson to resign, quoting [Leo Amery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Amery) calling on [Neville Chamberlain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain) to resign during the [Norway Debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norway_Debate) in 1940, and saying: "You have sat there too long for all the good you have done. In the name of God, go."[^cite_note-independent_1996225-510][^cite_note-bbc_60046632-511][^cite_note-inews_1411614-512]



An image of Johnson at a social gathering, from [Sue Gray's report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Gray's_report) into the [partygate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partygate) scandal

On 25 January, the [Metropolitan Police](192450.md) announced that they were commencing investigations into the Downing Street Parties.[^cite_note-bbc_60123850-513] An abbreviated version of the [Sue Gray report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Gray_report) into the controversy was released on 31 January, where Gray concluded there was a "failure of leadership" over the events that she had examined. The release of the full report was delayed pending the Metropolitan Police's investigation.[^cite_note-bbc_60210893-514]

In April 2022, Johnson was issued a [fixed penalty notice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed_penalty_notice) as police determined that he committed a criminal offence by breaching the COVID-19 lockdown regulations.[^cite_note-ft_20220412-515][^cite_note-theguardian_2022apr12_boris-516] Johnson therefore became the first prime minister in British history to have been sanctioned for breaking the law while in office.[^cite_note-Scott-2022-517]

According to Downing Street insiders, Johnson was involved in instigating a party on the occasion of [Lee Cain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Cain) leaving Number 10. What had begun as press office drinks became a party after Johnson arrived, gave a speech and poured drinks for staff. [Labour](19279158.md)'s deputy leader, [Angela Rayner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Rayner) said, "If the latest reports are true, it would mean that not only did the prime minister attend parties, but he had a hand in instigating at least one of them. He has deliberately misled the British people at every turn. The prime minister has demeaned his office."[^cite_note-thetimes_20220416-518][^cite_note-ft_20220417-519][^cite_note-bbc_61140292-520][^cite_note-theguardian_2022apr17_report-521] On 21 April, MPs voted to refer Johnson to the [Parliamentary Privileges Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commons_Select_Committee_of_Privileges) to investigate whether he knowingly misled Parliament.[^cite_note-bbc_61143220-522] [Steve Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Baker_\(politician\)) said Johnson's "marvellous contrition... only lasted as long as it took to get out of the headmaster's study".[^cite_note-sky_12595026-523]

Following the [May 2022 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_Kingdom_local_elections), many leading Conservatives in areas where the Conservatives had done badly blamed Johnson and calling on Johnson to resign.[^cite_note-theguardian_2022may06_local-524] On 6 June, [Graham Brady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_Brady) announced that the threshold for a vote on Johnson's leadership had been passed; the vote was scheduled for later that same day.[^cite_note-theguardian_2022jun06_boris-525]

On 3 March 2023, an interim report from the [Commons Select Committee of Privileges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commons_Select_Committee_of_Privileges) said there was evidence that "strongly suggests" breaches of coronavirus regulations would have been "obvious" to Johnson.[^cite_note-interimP-526] The report also said "There is evidence that those who were advising Mr Johnson about what to say to the press and in the House were themselves struggling to contend that some gatherings were within the rules".[^cite_note-interimP-526] Johnson said none of the evidence showed he "knowingly" misled Parliament.[^cite_note-interimP-526] The report stated that the Commons may have been misled on multiple occasions and Johnson "did not correct the statements \[at the] earliest opportunity".[^cite_note-interimP-526] The committee also stated that Johnson had "personal knowledge" over lockdown gatherings in No 10, which he could have disclosed.[^cite_note-interimP-526]

#### Starmer slur controversy



Johnson with [Keir Starmer](18681491.md) and former prime minister [Theresa May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theresa_May), 14 November 2021

While speaking in the House of Commons in January 2022, Johnson falsely blamed Starmer for the non-prosecution of the serial sex offender [Jimmy Savile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Savile) when Starmer was [Director of Public Prosecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director_of_Public_Prosecutions_\(England_and_Wales\)) in the [Crown Prosecution Service](383064.md). Starmer was DPP in the years immediately prior to Savile's death but there is no evidence he was involved in the decision to not have him prosecuted.[^cite_note-bbc_60213975-527] A few days later, Johnson defended his comments but conceded that Starmer "had nothing to do personally with those decisions" by the CPS not to investigate Savile.[^cite_note-sky_12532185-528]

#### Vote of confidence

Main article: [2022 vote of confidence in the Conservative Party leadership of Boris Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_vote_of_confidence_in_the_Conservative_Party_leadership_of_Boris_Johnson)

In the week prior to and throughout the [Platinum Jubilee of Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platinum_Jubilee_of_Elizabeth_II) in June 2022, it had been speculated that a vote of confidence in Johnson's leadership of the Conservative Party might soon occur.[^cite_note-reuters_20220531-529][^cite_note-itv_20220605_no-530] On 6 June 2022, the Conservative Party announced that Johnson would face a vote of confidence in his leadership of the party, after at least 54 Conservative MPs wrote no-confidence letters to Sir [Graham Brady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_Brady), the chairman of the [1922 Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1922_Committee). Johnson won the vote, with 211 in favour and 148 against.[^cite_note-bbc_61703422-531][^cite_note-MasonR-2022-532] The number of rebel MPs was larger than had been expected.[^cite_note-MasonR-2022-532] The result was described by Keir Starmer as the "beginning of the end" for Johnson's premiership.[^cite_note-independent_2094718-533]

#### June 2022 by-elections

Following heavy Conservative defeats in the 23 June 2022 by-elections in [Wakefield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Wakefield_by-election) and [Tiverton and Honiton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Tiverton_and_Honiton_by-election), former party leader Michael Howard called for Johnson to resign, saying: "\[Mr Johnson's] biggest asset has always been his ability to win votes but I'm afraid yesterday's results make it clear that he no longer has that ability."[^cite_note-bbc_61922077-534][^cite_note-independent_2108851-535]

[Oliver Dowden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Dowden), the Co-[Chairman of the Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_Conservative_Party), resigned saying: "somebody must take responsibility".[^cite_note-independent_2108377-536] Johnson announced that he had no intention of changing or resigning; senior Conservatives accused him of increasingly "delusional" behaviour.[^cite_note-theguardian_2022jun25_boris-537] On 26 June 2022 Johnson said: "At the moment I'm thinking actively about the third term and what could happen then, but I will review that when I get to it." He also claimed that he intended to stay as prime minister until the mid-2030s, although Number 10 later said that he had been joking.[^cite_note-archive_61939938-538]

#### Pincher scandal

Main article: [Chris Pincher scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Pincher_scandal)

Government Deputy Chief Whip [Chris Pincher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Pincher) resigned on 30 June 2022, saying he had "drunk far too much" the night before at the [Carlton Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlton_Club) in [St James's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_James's), London, and having "embarrassed myself and other people".[^cite_note-bbc_62014765-539] It was later alleged that he sexually assaulted two men,[^cite_note-sky2/7/2022-540] and he was suspended as an MP.[^cite_note-bbc_62014765-539] On 3 July 2022 six new allegations against Pincher emerged, involving behaviour over a decade.[^cite_note-bbc_62025612-541]

Johnson allegedly referred to Pincher as "handsy" and Cummings said Johnson joked about him being "Pincher by name, pincher by nature" in 2020, leading to calls for Johnson to explain how much he knew about Pincher's behaviour.[^cite_note-independent_2114609-542] Ministers initially said that Johnson was unaware of any specific complaints against Pincher when he was appointed as deputy chief whip. The BBC then reported, however, that an official complaint and subsequent investigation into Pincher, while he was at the Foreign Office (July 2019 to February 2020), had confirmed his misconduct, and that Johnson had been made aware at that time.[^cite_note-bbc_62040915-543] Sir [Simon McDonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_McDonald,_Baron_McDonald_of_Salford), former [Permanent Under-Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_Under-Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_Affairs), later said that the prime minister had been briefed "in person" about Pincher.[^cite_note-dailyrecord_27399080-544][^cite_note-inews_1723526-545]

#### Mass resignations

Main article: [July 2022 United Kingdom government crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2022_United_Kingdom_government_crisis)

On 5 July 2022, Sunak and Javid resigned within minutes of each other,[^cite_note-bbc_62054067-546] followed over the next 24 hours by 11 other ministers, as well as Conservative MPs from [parliamentary private secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_private_secretary) and other Government positions;[^cite_note-RESIG-547] other [backbenchers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backbencher) also withdrew their support for Johnson. Many of the MPs stated that the Pincher affair had led them to change their minds on the suitability of Johnson to be prime minister.[^cite_note-RES-548] It was reported on 6 July that Johnson could face another confidence vote, with members of the 1922 Committee considering changing the rules as soon as that evening to allow this to happen.[^cite_note-VONC-549] By 6 July, there had been a total of 31 resignations.[^cite_note-theguardian_2022jul06_the-550] As of May 2022, the government comprised 122 ministers.[^cite_note-instituteforgovernment_20190521-551]



Johnson announcing his resignation on 7 July 2022

#### Announcement of resignation

By the morning of 7 July, the newly installed [Chancellor of the Exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer), [Nadhim Zahawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadhim_Zahawi), publicly stated his belief that Johnson should resign. Within hours, the BBC and other media reported Johnson's intention to resign. Johnson announced his resignation at 12.30 pm.[^cite_note-bbc_62070422-552][^cite_note-cnbc_20220707_boris-553][^cite_note-abc_101212040-554]

> I know that there will be many who are relieved, but perhaps quite a few who will be disappointed. And I want you to know how sad I am to give up the best job in the world – but them's the breaks.

Upon reports of his resignation, the [pound sterling](270673.md) temporarily strengthened in value, and UK stocks rose.[^cite_note-bloomberg_20220707_pound-555] He remained as prime minister until September, while the Conservative Party chose a new leader.[^cite_note-bbc_62072419-556] On 5 September 2022, it was announced that [Liz Truss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liz_Truss) had won the [Conservative leadership election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July%E2%80%93September_2022_Conservative_Party_leadership_election). She became prime minister the next day.[^cite_note-independent_2160000-557][^cite_note-theguardian_2022sep06_liz-558] During his farewell speech outside 10 Downing Street on 6 September 2022, Johnson referred to the Roman statesman [Cincinnatus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnatus). Some commentators noted that, while, as Johnson said, Cincinnatus returned to his plough, he was also later recalled to power.[^cite_note-theguardian_2022sep06_boris-559]

### Environmental policies

In November 2020 Johnson announced a 10-point plan for a "green industrial revolution", to include ending the sale of petrol and [diesel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diesel_engine) cars and vans by 2030,[^cite_note-561] quadrupling the amount of [offshore wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_the_United_Kingdom) capacity within a decade, funding emissions-cutting proposals, and spurning a proposed [green post-COVID-19 recovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_recovery).[^cite_note-theguardian_2020nov17_the-562] In 2021 the Johnson government announced plans to cut [carbon emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions_by_the_United_Kingdom) by 78% by 2035.[^cite_note-bbc_56843367-563]

Johnson announced that the UK would join the Global Methane Pledge to cut [methane emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methane_emissions) by 30% by 2030 at the [COP26](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COP26) summit, which the UK hosted.[^cite_note-theguardian_2021sep17_us-564][^cite_note-Auto4S-32-565] Before the summit, representatives of [Greenpeace](12233.md) and [Friends of the Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friends_of_the_Earth) criticised Johnson's comments on plans to introduce "enforceable limits" on carbon emissions for other countries, which they accused of being unsubstantive,[^cite_note-independent_9289116-566] and his government faced criticism from environmental groups for cutting taxes on domestic air travel, given the [environmental impact of aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_effects_of_aviation_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-independent_1946258-567]

In April 2022, Johnson announced that eight more [nuclear reactors](22151.md) would be built on existing [nuclear power plant](207347.md) sites and called for an expansion in wind energy.[^cite_note-apnews_070111-568] Under these plans, up to 95% of the UK's electricity could come from [low-carbon power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-carbon_power) sources by 2030.[^cite_note-bbc_61010605-569]

### Foreign policy

Further information: [List of international prime ministerial trips made by Boris Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_prime_ministerial_trips_made_by_Boris_Johnson)



Johnson with US president Donald Trump at the [G7 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/45th_G7_summit) in [Biarritz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biarritz), 26 August 2019

Johnson supported the [European Union–Mercosur Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union%E2%80%93Mercosur_Free_Trade_Agreement),[^cite_note-Auto4S-33-570] which would form one of the world's largest free trade areas.[^cite_note-dw_50154578-571] Johnson's government placed importance on the "[Special Relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Relationship)" with the [United States](1814362.md).[^cite_note-cnn_20201017_politics-572][^cite_note-Auto4S-34-573] In 2022, his government introduced an [asylum deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rwanda_asylum_plan) whereby people entering the UK illegally would be sent to Rwanda.[^cite_note-aljazeera_2022414_uk-574]



Johnson and Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) at the [Berlin Conference on Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_peace_process), 19 January 2020



Johnson and Canadian Prime Minister [Justin Trudeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Trudeau) at the [47th G7 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/47th_G7_summit) in Cornwall, 11 June 2021



Johnson with Polish troops and Poland's PM [Mateusz Morawiecki](48505487.md) in Warsaw, 10 February 2022

#### Hong Kong and China

Johnson said in July 2019 that his government would be very "pro-China" in an interview with the Hong Kong broadcaster [Phoenix TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_TV). He voiced support for Chinese president [Xi Jinping](2017814.md)'s infrastructure investment effort, the [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative), and promised to keep the United Kingdom "the most open economy in Europe" for [Chinese investment](19284336.md#Chinese_investment_abroad).[^cite_note-yahoo_065100165-575]

In June 2020 Johnson announced that if China were to continue pursuing the [Hong Kong national security law](64033197.md), the UK would offer 350,000 [Hong Kong residents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hongkongers) who are [British National (Overseas) passport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_National_\(Overseas\)_passport) holders, and 2.6 million other eligible individuals, the chance to move to the UK.[^cite_note-bbc_53246899-576] China accused the UK of interfering in its internal affairs.[^cite_note-Auto4S-35-577]

Johnson declined to describe the Chinese government's [treatment of the Uyghur people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Uyghurs_in_China) as "genocide", despite use of the term by the United States.[^cite_note-genocide-578] Johnson's government argued that genocide should be decided by the International Criminal Court.[^cite_note-bloomberg_20210322_johnson-579] Nevertheless, he called what is happening to the [Uyghurs](31783.md) in Xinjiang as "utterly abhorrent".[^cite_note-genocide-578]

The UK joined the [AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS) defence pact with the United States and Australia in September 2021. The pact was denounced by China[^cite_note-bbc_58582573-580] and caused a French backlash, as it usurped existing [plans for Australia to procure French submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack-class_submarine).[^cite_note-theguardian_2021sep22_aukus-581]

On 29 October 2025, The Guardian newspaper revealed that Boris Johnson approved the controversial China's London super-embassy proposal in 2018, a project that remains in limbo seven years later.[^cite_note-theguardian_2025oct29_boris-582]

#### Afghanistan

On 8 July 2021 the day after saying he was "apprehensive" about the future of Afghanistan following what was then the [impending withdrawal of US troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Afghanistan_\(2020%E2%80%932021\)), while announcing the near completion of British troop withdrawal from Afghanistan, Johnson expressed the view that there was "no military path to victory for the [Taliban](30635.md)".[^cite_note-livemint_116257471976-583][^cite_note-Taliban-584] Following the [fall of Kabul to the Taliban](68481047.md), he blamed the United States for the crisis,[^cite_note-theguardian_2021aug16_afghanistan-585][^cite_note-theweek_20210816_trouble-586] saying that [NATO](21133.md) alliance members "could not continue this US-led mission, a mission conceived and executed in support of America, without American logistics, without US air power and without American might".[^cite_note-Auto4S-36-587]

#### UK–EU trade negotiation

Main articles: [Trade deal negotiation between the UK and EU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_deal_negotiation_between_the_UK_and_EU) and [EU–UK Trade and Cooperation Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU%E2%80%93UK_Trade_and_Cooperation_Agreement)

Following the formal [withdrawal from the European Union](41688778.md) in January 2020, Johnson's government entered [trade negotiations with the EU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_negotiation_between_the_UK_and_the_EU).[^cite_note-Mueller-2021-588] [Fisheries was a major topic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_for_finance) of the negotiations.[^cite_note-Mueller-2021-588][^cite_note-reuters_idUSKBN29415G-589] On 16 October 2020 Johnson said that the UK "must get ready" for no trade deal with the EU.[^cite_note-theguardian_2020oct16_uk-590] It was announced on 24 December 2020[^cite_note-theguardian_2020dec24_uk-591] that the [EU–UK Trade and Cooperation Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU%E2%80%93UK_Trade_and_Cooperation_Agreement) had been reached; it came into force formally on 1 May. A [fisheries dispute between the UK and France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Jersey_dispute) occurred shortly afterwards. Introduction of new UK border checks were delayed until 2022 to minimise the disruption caused by the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-Mueller-2021-588]

In May 2022, Johnson readied a draft that would unilaterally change parts of the [Northern Ireland Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Protocol), citing issues with medical supplies and cuts in VAT. One of the thornier points of contention involves safety regulations for food and plants, where the British government is opposed to a closer alignment with existing EU regulations. The EU rebuffed the idea of changing the text of the treaty to accommodate the British. A unilateral override by the UK would be tantamount to a breach of the agreement. As Johnson sought a more conciliatory tone, sources within the government began to stress that the draft is designed to be an "insurance policy" and would take years to become law.[^cite_note-thetimes_20220516-592]

#### Russia and Ukraine



Johnson walks on a street of war-hit [Kyiv](585629.md) along with [President of Ukraine](20688479.md) [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) on 10 April 2022.

In November 2021 Johnson warned that the European Union faces "a choice" between "sticking up for Ukraine" and approving the [Nord Stream 2](50335419.md) natural gas pipeline from Russia to Europe.[^cite_note-ft_1798431-593]

During the [prelude to the Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prelude_to_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), Johnson's government warned the Russian Government not to invade [Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas).[^cite_note-bbc_60204847-594][^cite_note-co_20220131_boris-595][^cite_note-bbc_60159622-596] Johnson and [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) agreed in a phone call to work towards a "peaceful resolution".[^cite_note-aljazeera_20220202-597] On 1 February 2022, Johnson arrived in [Kyiv](585629.md) on a diplomatic visit.[^cite_note-theguardian_2022feb01_boris-598] He called the presence of the [Russian Armed Forces](25709.md) near the [Russia–Ukraine border](36430484.md) "the biggest security crisis that Europe has faced for decades".[^cite_note-rferl-599] The Kremlin denied that it wanted to attack Ukraine.[^cite_note-rferl-599] On 20 February 2022, Johnson warned that Russia is planning the "biggest war in Europe since [1945](32927.md)" as Putin intends to invade and encircle Kyiv.[^cite_note-bbc_60448162-600] On 21 February 2022, Johnson condemned [Russia's diplomatic recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_the_Donetsk_People's_Republic_and_the_Luhansk_People's_Republic_by_the_Russian_Federation) of two [self-proclaimed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-declared_states) republics in Donbas.[^cite_note-reuters_idUSKBN2KQ1TS-601]

On 24 February 2022, Johnson condemned the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), and ensured the UK joined in [international sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) on Russian banks and [oligarchs](1209554.md).[^cite_note-bbc_60515626-602][^cite_note-bloomberg_20220629_uk-603] He later announced the UK would phase out [Russian oil](10450931.md) by the end of 2022.[^cite_note-independent_2031837-604]

On 9 April 2022, Johnson travelled to Kyiv and met the [President of Ukraine](20688479.md), [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md).[^cite_note-theguardian_2022apr09_boris-605] On 16 April 2022, Russia's [Ministry for Foreign Affairs](4432213.md) banned Johnson and a number of senior British politicians from visiting Russia, saying that Britain aimed to isolate Russia politically and supply "the Kyiv regime with lethal weapons and coordinating similar efforts on the part of NATO".[^cite_note-bbc_61126391-606]

Within Ukraine, Johnson is praised by many as a supporter of anti-Russian sanctions and [military aid for Ukraine](70175610.md).[^cite_note-thetimes_20220424-607] On 3 May, Johnson [virtually addressed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_conferencing) the [Ukrainian Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Parliament), becoming the first world leader to speak in Ukraine since the invasion. He pledged an extra £300m in military aid to Ukraine, praised Ukraine's resistance to Russia as its "finest hour" and said that the West had been "too slow to grasp what was actually happening" prior to Russia's invasion.[^cite_note-sky_12604138-608][^cite_note-bbc_61297478-609] In July 2022, Johnson warned that it would be a mistake to cease fire and [freeze the conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_negotiations_in_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-euronews_20220619_it-610] In August 2022, Johnson blamed Vladimir Putin for the emerging [global energy crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%93present_global_energy_crisis).[^cite_note-ft_20220828-611]

## Post-premiership (2022–present)

|  | This section **contains an excessive amount of intricate [detail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:DETAIL)**. Please help [improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boris_Johnson\&action=edit) by [spinning off](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Content_forks#Article_spinoffs:_.22Summary_style.22_meta-articles_and_summary_sections) or [relocating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Handling_trivia#Recommendations_for_handling_trivia) relevant information and removing excessive detail that goes against [Wikipedia's inclusion policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:What_Wikipedia_is_not). *(May 2024)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |



Johnson leaving Number 10 Downing Street on his last day as prime minister.



Johnson with US Representative [John Rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Rose_\(Tennessee_politician\)) in 2023



Johnson with [William Lai](22609914.md), [President of Taiwan](61155.md), in 2025

After stepping down as party leader and prime minister, Johnson reverted to being an ordinary backbench MP.[^cite_note-sbs_20220722-612][^cite_note-people_20220912-613] Following the [death of Queen Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Elizabeth_II), Johnson took part in [Charles III](125248.md)'s [Accession Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_accession_of_Charles_III), and many other funeral-related events.[^cite_note-inews_1842612-614][^cite_note-theguardian_20220919_all-615][^cite_note-616] Johnson has given several speeches since the end of his premiership. In December 2022, it was reported that Johnson had made £1m from speeches since his resignation.[^cite_note-theguardian_20221214_boris-617]

After [Liz Truss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liz_Truss) announced her resignation as Conservative Party leader on 20 October 2022, Johnson sought support from MPs to run in the subsequent [leadership election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2022_Conservative_Party_leadership_election), and received support from several cabinet members.[^cite_note-BBC2110-618] Three days later, he announced that he would not stand, stating that he would not have enough support from MPs to govern effectively.[^cite_note-\:8-619]

After his former chancellor [Rishi Sunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishi_Sunak) was elected unopposed as party leader and prime minister, Johnson congratulated him and urged Conservatives to give Sunak "their full and wholehearted support.[^cite_note-\:6-620]

In May 2023, Johnson was referred to the police by the Cabinet Office regarding previously unknown potential breaches of COVID regulations between June 2020 and May 2021,[^cite_note-ChequersPoliceBBC-621] to which Johnson's office issued a statement criticising the "unfounded suggestions" which "has all the hallmarks of yet another politically motivated stitch-up".[^cite_note-ChequersPoliceBBC-621]

Johnson still faced a [Privileges Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commons_Select_Committee_of_Privileges) investigation as to whether he deliberately misled Parliament over gatherings during Covid lockdowns.[^cite_note-resignationJune23-622] After receiving a draft of the investigation's report, he resigned from Parliament.[^cite_note-nytimes_20230609-623][^cite_note-sky_12899673-624] The committee found that Johnson had deliberately misled MPs and therefore concluded that he had committed contempt of Parliament.[^cite_note-PrivCommittee-625] The recommendation from the committee was to suspend the former Prime Minister for more than ten days.[^cite_note-PrivCommittee-625] Johnson subsequently accused the committee of a witch hunt against him.[^cite_note-resignationJune23-622] Following Johnson's reaction to the report, including "impugning the committee", they added that they would have recommended a suspension of 90 days instead.[^cite_note-PrivCommittee-625] His resignation triggered a [by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Uxbridge_and_South_Ruislip_by-election) in his seat.[^cite_note-resignationJune23-622]

On 9 June 2023, the publication of his [2022 Prime Minister's Resignation Honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Prime_Minister's_Resignation_Honours_\(Boris_Johnson\)) led to a public feud with Sunak.[^cite_note-bbc_65864011-626][^cite_note-bbc_65876723-627] Johnson supporter [Nadine Dorries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadine_Dorries) announced that she was resigning as an MP due to not being included as a peer on the honours list.[^cite_note-sky_12901468-628] The same day, after receiving a confidential report from a committee of the [House of Commons](13828.md) that was looking into whether he had lied to Parliament over lockdown-breaking gatherings, Johnson announced his resignation as MP.[^cite_note-sky_12835826-629] His resignation statement said he is "not alone in thinking that there is a witch-hunt under way, to take revenge for Brexit and ultimately to reverse the 2016 referendum result".[^cite_note-theguardian_2023jun09_it-630]

&#x20;On 15 June, the Commons Privileges Committee published their report, which concluded that Johnson lied to and deliberately misled the House of Commons over Partygate, misled the Committee themselves during the hearing, and acted in contempt of the Committee itself through a "campaign of abuse and intimidation".[^cite_note-theguardian_2023jun15_johnson-631] The report noted that had Johnson still been an MP, the committee would have recommended he be suspended from Parliament for 90 days.[^cite_note-bbc_65876914-632][^cite_note-Auto4S-38-633] The contents of the report represented a recommendation to the House of Commons,[^cite_note-sky_12903366-634] which accepted the report by 354 votes to seven.[^cite_note-independent_2360396-635][^cite_note-bbc_65953605-636]



Johnson with students and lecturers of [Taras Shevchenko National University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taras_Shevchenko_National_University) of Kyiv, Ukraine, 22 January 2023

On 16 June, Johnson was unveiled as a new columnist for the *[Daily Mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily_Mail)*. The news website *[Politico Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politico_Europe)* reported that Johnson would be paid a "very-high six-figure sum".[^cite_note-Auto4S-39-637][^cite_note-independent_2360572-638] Johnson reportedly informed the [Advisory Committee on Business Appointments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advisory_Committee_on_Business_Appointments) half an hour before the columnist assignment was publicly announced. The committee ruled that Johnson committed a "clear breach" of the rules since he had not sought its advice on the matter within an appropriate timeframe.[^cite_note-sky_12903648-639][^cite_note-Scott_2023_z305-640]

In October 2023, Johnson announced he would join the television channel [GB News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GB_News) as a commentator and programme maker for the [next general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election) and [US presidential election](59568546.md).[^cite_note-bbc_67242822-641][^cite_note-theguardian_2023oct27_boris-642]

Johnson condemned [Hamas](13913.md)' attack [on Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_October_attacks), saying "there can be no moral equivalence between the terrorism of Hamas and the actions of the Israeli Defense Forces". He rejected calls for a ceasefire in the [Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_humanitarian_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)) during the [Gaza war](74998519.md), and criticised [pro-Palestinian protests in the UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_war_protests_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-timesofisrael_20231105-643] On 5 November 2023, Johnson visited Israel to express solidarity.[^cite_note-independent_2441887-644] In February 2024, Johnson had a private meeting with the president of Venezuela, [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md).[^cite_note-bbc_68526317-645]

On 2 May 2024 Johnson was turned away from his polling station for the [Thames Valley Police and Crime Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_England_and_Wales_police_and_crime_commissioner_elections), after forgetting to bring [valid photographic identification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_Act_2022), a requirement of the [Elections Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_Act_2001), which Johnson introduced while in office. When he arrived he had nothing to prove his identity except the sleeve of his copy of *[Prospect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prospect_\(magazine\))* magazine, on which his name and address had been printed. He said that when he returned a few minutes later, with his [driving licence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driving_licence_in_the_United_Kingdom), he was then able to vote.[^cite_note-theguardian_2024may04_boris-646] During the [2024 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election), Johnson campaigned for the Conservatives, who lost the election in a landslide to Labour.[^cite_note-sky_13162656-647]

Johnson's memoir *[Unleashed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unleashed_\(memoir\))* was released in October 2024.[^cite_note-Auto4S-40-648][^cite_note-co_20240620_boris-649][^cite_note-theguardian_2023jan16_boris-650]

In 2025, following [Keir Starmer](18681491.md)'s decision to recognise a Palestinian state, Johnson said the move was "ridiculous" and aimed for political benefit among voters as well as motivated by inner party politics.[^cite_note-\:1-651] Johnson also said that a Palestinian state should not be recognised because it has no clearly defined borders and its government is unable to control its borders as well as being ruled by Hamas, which Johnson described as "fascist".[^cite_note-\:1-651]

In September 2026, Johnson attended the Yalta European Strategy, an international conference in [Kyiv](585629.md), [Ukraine](31750.md) along with several European national security advisors including [Jonathan Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Powell_\(civil_servant\)). On their return journey, by train to the [Polish](22936.md) border, the following locomotive was targeted by [Russian drones](25391.md), on the same line that they had earlier travelled. Johnson, writing on [X](9988187.md) described [Putin](32817.md)'s attack, as "the kind of random and senseless attack Ukrainians are enduring every day - even on civilian railways".[^cite_note-652]

## Political positions and ideology

Main article: [Political positions of Boris Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_Boris_Johnson)



Johnson at a demonstration against hospital closures with Liberal Democrat MP [John Hemming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hemming_\(politician\)) (left) and Conservative MP [Graham Stuart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_Stuart_\(politician\)) (centre) in March 2006

> \[I am] free-market, tolerant, broadly libertarian (though perhaps not ultra-libertarian), inclined to see the merit of traditions, anti-regulation, pro-immigrant, pro-standing on your own two feet, pro-alcohol, pro-hunting, pro-motorist and ready to defend to the death the right of [Glenn Hoddle to believe in reincarnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenn_Hoddle#Dismissal_from_England_job).

— Boris Johnson, 2011

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPurnell2011225-133]

Ideologically, Johnson has been described as a "[One-Nation Tory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-Nation_Tory)".[^cite_note-thetimes_20191215-653][^cite_note-Auto4S-41-654] Political scientists have described Johnson's political positions as ambiguous and contradictory, encompassing [nativist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)), authoritarian and [free market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_market) tendencies on the one hand, and one-nation [liberal conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_conservatism) on the other.[^cite_note-Hayton_2021-655] Some scholars have questioned Johnson's commitment to one-nation conservativism, instead characterising his ideology as flexible and populist.[^cite_note-Auto4S-42-656][^cite_note-politicshome_20220211-657] Purnell stated that Johnson regularly changed his opinion on political issues, commenting on what she perceived to be "an ideological emptiness beneath the staunch Tory exterior".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPurnell20112-658]

During his tenure as mayor, Johnson gained a reputation as "a liberal, centre-ground politician", according to *[Business Insider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_Insider)*.[^cite_note-businessinsider_20190523-659] In 2012, the political scientist [Tony Travers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Travers) described Johnson as "a fairly classic—that is, small-state—mildly [eurosceptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euroscepticism_in_the_United_Kingdom) Conservative" who also embraced "modern social liberalism".[^cite_note-Channel4-660] *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* stated that while mayor, Johnson blended economic and social liberalism,[^cite_note-theguardian_2008dec04_boris-661] with *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* saying that in doing so Johnson "transcends his Tory identity" and adopts a more libertarian perspective.[^cite_note-economist_21578666-662] According to political scientist Richard Hayton, Johnson's premiership was about Brexit, which served as a "national cause". Johnson evoked the discourse of [popular sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_sovereignty) and [anti-establishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-establishment) populism to portray Parliament as seeking to "sabotage" Brexit, and in doing so, presented himself "as the true representative of 'the people'".[^cite_note-Hayton_2021-655][^cite_note-Alexandre-Collier_2022-663]

Scholars of [comparative politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comparative_politics) have drawn comparisons between Johnson and other populist leaders such as Donald Trump and [Viktor Orban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Orban).[^cite_note-Alexandre-Collier_2022-663] Some commentators have likened Johnson's political style to [Trumpism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trumpism),[^cite_note-Chadwick-2016-664][^cite_note-Greenslade-2016-665][^cite_note-atlantic202110-666] although others have argued that Johnson's stance on matters such as social policy, immigration and free trade is liberal.[^cite_note-washingtonpost_20190723_dont-667] Johnson biographer Gimson wrote that Johnson is economically and socially "a genuine liberal", although he retains a "Tory element" through his "love of existing institutions, and a recognition of the inevitability of hierarchy".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGimson2012342-668] In 2019, reacting to reports in [*The Sun*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sun_\(United_Kingdom\)), that Johnson had told cabinet colleagues he was "basically a Brexity Hezza", former deputy leader of the Conservative Party [Michael Heseltine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Heseltine) wrote: "I fear that any traces of liberal conservatism that still exist within the prime minister have long since been captured by the rightwing, foreigner-bashing, inward-looking view of the world that has come to characterise his fellow Brexiters."[^cite_note-theguardian_2019sep12_boris-669]

### Environment



Johnson spoke about climate action at the [COP26 climate summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference) in Glasgow on 1 November 2021.

Johnson initially expressed [climate sceptical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_sceptic) views in several newspaper columns.[^cite_note-BBC_News-2022-670][^cite_note-The_Week_UK-2021-671][^cite_note-Ward-2021-672] As Mayor of London from 2008 to 2016, Boris Johnson initiated several tree-planting programs to enhance the city's green spaces. His flagship pledge was to plant 10,000 street trees by 2012.[^cite_note-bbc_11896520-673][^cite_note-Auto4S-43-674]

In 2019 and 2020 Johnson expressed support for the UK to have ["net-zero" greenhouse gas emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_neutrality) by 2050[^cite_note-newscientist_2206302-675] and spoke about increasing ambition for mitigating climate change through [carbon capture and storage](3679268.md)[^cite_note-co_20200924_boris-676] and a [renewable energy transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_transition).[^cite_note-bbc_54441613-677] During the [2021 United Nations Climate Change Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference), Johnson called for greater efforts towards [climate change mitigation](2119179.md),[^cite_note-bbc_59114871-678] and welcomed the prospect of [coal phase-out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_phase-out).[^cite_note-bbc_59284505-679]

### Immigration and the European Union



Johnson with EU Commission president [Jean-Claude Juncker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Claude_Juncker), 16 September 2019

Purnell believed it was the influence of Johnson's maternal family that led to him developing "a genuine abhorrence of racial discrimination".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPurnell201125-680] In 2003, Johnson said, "I am not by any means an ultra-[Eurosceptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euroscepticism_in_the_United_Kingdom). In some ways, I am a bit of a fan of the European Union. If we did not have one, we would invent something like it."[^cite_note-businessinsider-681] As mayor, Johnson was known as a supporter of [immigration](1247786.md).[^cite_note-businessinsider-681] From 2009, he advocated a referendum on Britain's EU membership.[^cite_note-businessinsider-681][^cite_note-canterbury_20190301-682][^cite_note-independent_7363781-683]
